\noindent{\sffamily\bfseries\fontsize{18}{22}\selectfont\raggedright\color{ink}
MERRA21C-ML: Conditional Flow Matching for Multivariate Kilometer-Scale Downscaling of GEOS-FP\par}
\vspace{0.7em}
\noindent{\sffamily\large\bfseries
Abdullah A. Fahad$^{1,2,*}$,
Katherine H. Breen$^{1,3}$,
Carl Malings$^{1,3}$,
William M. Putman$^{1}$,
Caterina Mogno$^{1,4}$, and

\vspace{0.4em}
\noindent{\sffamily\small\color{muted}

$^{*}$\,Correspondence: \href{mailto:a.fahad@nasa.gov}{a.fahad@nasa.gov}, \href{mailto:afahadabdullah@gmail.com}{afahadabdullah@gmail.com}%

\vspace{0.6em}
\noindent{\sffamily\small\color{accent}Research manuscript draft \enspace\textbullet\enspace 14 October 2026}\par
\vspace{0.6em}
\noindent{\sffamily\bfseries\large\color{navy}Abstract}\par
Coarse atmospheric analyses constrain large-scale weather but do not uniquely determine kilometer-scale precipitation and near-surface variability. We present \model{}, a conditional flow-matching framework that jointly generates precipitation, 2-m temperature and specific humidity, surface pressure, and the two 10-m wind components over the contiguous United States. The model maps GEOS-FP fields on a $0.25^\circ\times0.3125^\circ$ output grid to a $1059\times1799$ Lambert conformal grid using high-resolution GEOS replay fields as the learning reference. Building on CorrDiff's regression-to-generative strategy, a hybrid representation generates precipitation directly in square-root space while representing temperature, pressure, and winds as residual corrections to a frozen regression estimate and humidity as a coarse-field residual. Qualitative development comparisons indicate that direct precipitation generation better preserves organized storm patterns than the earlier residual formulation; sharper moisture features remain harder to recover. Local predictors, previous-hour coarse conditions, static surface information, and broad spatial context condition a shared U-Net. Overlapping tiles exchange information through a common velocity field at every integration step, allowing full-domain sampling through tile-sized network evaluations. A bounded spectral filter enhances fine-scale structure without retraining, and a packed archive reduces repeated training-data access. Multiple noise draws produce conditional downscaling ensembles from one coarse input. In an exploratory evaluation of three selected held-out hours with eight members, 64 integration steps, and spectral post-processing, empirical continuous ranked probability score improves by 20.4\% for precipitation, 38.2\% for temperature, 95.5\% for surface pressure, 28.3--29.8\% for the wind components, and 28.6\% for humidity relative to interpolated GEOS-FP. Precipitation ensemble-mean root-mean-square error is slightly higher than the coarse baseline, and central empirical 90\% intervals cover only 57.1--76.1\% of reference values across the six fields. These case diagnostics establish neither population-wide skill nor observational accuracy. They motivate a full-domain evaluation that separates conditional fidelity, spatial structure, and calibration, with particular attention to precipitation, post-processing, and the restricted held-out season.

\vspace{0.8em}
\noindent{\sffamily\bfseries\color{accent}Key contributions}\par
\begin{itemize}
\item A variable-specific extension of regression-conditioned generation: direct square-root precipitation with residual temperature, pressure, wind, and humidity fields, learned jointly through conditional flow matching.
\item A testable finding from development experiments: more organized precipitation with direct generation, alongside persistent difficulty recovering sharp humidity features.
\item Sharpening without retraining, evaluated as a paired raw-versus-filtered ensemble comparison, with accuracy, structure, and calibration assessed together.
\end{itemize}
\vspace{0.5em}
\noindent\textbf{Keywords:} statistical downscaling; flow matching; generative modeling; GEOS-FP; precipitation; multivariate ensembles.
\vfill

Numerical statements refer only to the identified three-case diagnostic archive. Author declarations and the complete evaluation remain to be finalized.}
\twocolumn\raggedbottom

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}
Weather-sensitive decisions often depend on atmospheric variability at scales finer than those represented in distributed global analysis products. Terrain, coastlines, and land--water contrasts influence near-surface temperature, humidity, pressure, and wind. Precipitation adds a more difficult requirement: its occurrence, intensity, and spatial organization can vary sharply within a coarse grid cell. Interpolation places coarse information on a finer grid, but does not reconstruct the conditional distribution of unresolved weather. A useful downscaling system must therefore address both predictable local adjustments and uncertainty in fine-scale structure.

Statistical downscaling learns relationships between large-scale predictors and finer reference fields. Deep neural networks extend this approach by learning spatially structured and nonlinear relationships. Deterministic models can recover systematic terrain-dependent signals, yet a pointwise squared-error objective favors the conditional mean. When several locations of a rainband or frontal boundary are compatible with the predictors, their average can be spatially smooth even if every physically plausible realization is sharp. Stochastic downscaling instead describes a distribution of possible fine-scale outcomes. Generative precipitation studies have demonstrated the relevance of this distinction using coarse weather-model predictors and radar references \citep{harris2022}.

Diffusion models provide an effective framework for learning such conditional distributions. CorrDiff combines a deterministic estimate with generative residual corrections for multivariate kilometer-scale atmospheric downscaling \citep{mardani2025}. This decomposition allocates predictable structure to regression and unresolved variability to a stochastic model. Related work uses video diffusion to represent temporal dependence in precipitation downscaling \citep{srivastava2024}. These developments establish strong methodological precedents, while leaving practical questions about mixed-variable representations, large-domain sampling, and calibration against appropriate references.

Flow matching offers a complementary route to conditional generation. Rather than fitting a sequence of noise-removal transitions, it learns a velocity field that transports a simple distribution toward the data distribution \citep{lipman2023}. A linear interpolation between independent Gaussian noise and data gives a simple regression target and connects naturally to rectified-flow formulations \citep{liu2023}. Training does not require solving the generative ordinary differential equation. Sampling does require numerical integration, whose accuracy and cost must be evaluated for the chosen architecture and spatial assembly procedure.

Applying these ideas over the contiguous United States (CONUS) presents three challenges. First, precipitation is nonnegative and strongly skewed, whereas signed wind components and temperature departures are naturally expressed as residuals. A uniform representation need not be appropriate for every variable. Second, a continental grid contains nearly two million cells per field. Patchwise learning is practical, but independently generated patches can disagree at their boundaries. Third, attractive maps do not establish reliable probabilistic information. A model can produce sharper structures while misplacing events, or improve a proper score while remaining underdispersive.

We develop a deep-learning amendment of coarse atmospheric analysis fields that can also serve as a downscaling component downstream of numerical weather prediction (NWP), subject to separate validation on forecast inputs. This paper describes \model{} v4.1, a six-field conditional generator driven by current and previous-hour GEOS-FP information. Its main methodological choices are a direct square-root precipitation representation, residual state variables, local and broad-context conditioning, and velocity-level synchronization during tiled sampling. Its implementation contribution is a packed archive that avoids repeatedly constructing large context crops. The name identifies the project; the experiment reported here uses GEOS-FP inputs, not MERRA-2, which is a distinct retrospective analysis product \citep{gelaro2017}.

We ask whether this construction can provide useful conditional ensembles without conflating fine-scale variability with deterministic accuracy. The available numerical evidence comprises three selected held-out hours, rather than a completed climatological evaluation. We report those results explicitly, identify their restrictions, and specify the additional comparisons needed to establish general skill. Section~\ref{sec:data} defines the data and learning task; Section~\ref{sec:method} describes the model; Section~\ref{sec:evaluation} defines verification; Sections~\ref{sec:results} and \ref{sec:discussion} present the preliminary findings and their implications.

\subsection{Relationship to CorrDiff and research hypotheses}\label{sec:corrdiff}
CorrDiff provides the central precedent: a deterministic regression estimates predictable fine-scale structure and a diffusion model generates residual variability \citep{mardani2025}. Here the generative stage learns a conditional transport velocity through flow matching, and precipitation is exempted from residual subtraction. The original CorrDiff experiment includes radar reflectivity, so its radar synthesis result is not itself a direct precipitation-rate comparison. A matched benchmark must use the same reference variables, temporal support, and data partitions.

The principal scientific question is whether the hybrid target representation improves the organization of intermittent precipitation while retaining useful state-variable corrections. Our development experiments suggest more coherent storm patterns when precipitation is generated directly. Because those comparisons involve earlier model generations, this observation motivates a controlled ablation rather than an isolated causal attribution to the target transform. A second question is whether a bounded spectral adjustment improves spatial detail without degrading probability scores, physical amounts, or uncertainty calibration, particularly for humidity.

Ensemble generation is a practical advantage over a single deterministic estimate and is shared with CorrDiff. Later CorrDiff implementations also use patch-based multidiffusion to extend generation across large domains \citep{sui2025}; overlap synchronization is therefore not a unique contribution of this work. Our implementation applies synchronized velocity assembly to the six-field flow, with broad context and a packed loader. We distinguish these implementation contributions from the representation hypothesis and make no claim of priority for flow matching, ensemble downscaling, or spectral filtering in general.

\section{Data and learning task}\label{sec:data}
\subsection{Coarse predictors and fine reference}
The coarse predictors are GEOS Forward Processing (GEOS-FP) diagnostic fields distributed on a regular latitude--longitude grid with $0.25^\circ$ latitude and $0.3125^\circ$ longitude spacing. This is the output grid, not a statement that the underlying GEOS dynamical model integrates at that spacing. NASA's GEOS-FP documentation distinguishes the model grid, output grid, and time-averaged diagnostic collections \citep{geosfp}. The input collections used here provide hourly surface and flux fields centered at 30 minutes past each hour.

The high-resolution learning reference is the GEOS experiment \code{Feature-c2160_L137}, documented in the project archive as a GEOSgcm-v11.5.1 replay to GEOS-FP. Its CONUS output is stored on a Lambert conformal conic (LCC) grid with 1059 rows and 1799 columns. The experiment is named CONUS02km, while local documentation also describes nominal spacings of approximately 2--3 km. We therefore use ``kilometer-scale'' throughout and retain grid-cell area and geolocation for physical calculations. Table~\ref{tab:data} and Fig.~\ref{fig:data} reserve the data inventory and domain overview. The final figure axes must use distances computed from the archived geometry, rather than treating an experiment name as a verified constant grid spacing.

The reference consists of model output. It is neither an independent observation network nor a direct measurement of atmospheric truth. Because the high-resolution experiment is replayed to GEOS-FP, coarse and fine fields share large-scale information. This relationship is useful for learning conditional fine-scale variability, but limits any inference about performance against the real atmosphere. We use ``reference'' for the high-resolution fields and reserve observational validation for separate measurements.

\tableslot{2.6cm}{Data sources and target definitions. Reserved for the GEOS-FP and high-resolution GEOS collections, variables, units, spatial support, temporal support, and available timestamps. Distinguish the distributed GEOS-FP grid from its model grid, and instantaneous state targets from approximate hourly precipitation means. Table body intentionally blank.}{tab:data}

\subsection{Six target variables and temporal support}
At valid time $n$, the generated field is
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{Y}_n=(T_n,P_n,p_n,u_n,v_n,q_n),
\end{equation}
where $T$ is 2-m temperature, $P$ is total precipitation rate, $p$ is surface pressure, $u$ and $v$ are the 10-m wind components, and $q$ is 2-m specific humidity. Native units are K, $\rainunit$, Pa, $\windunit$, $\windunit$, and kg\,kg$^{-1}$, respectively. Temperature differences may equivalently be reported in $^\circ$C; pressure and humidity are displayed in hPa and $\qrate$ when convenient. Wind speed is a derived quantity, $\sqrt{u^2+v^2}$, computed for each member before forming ensemble summaries.

GEOS-FP precipitation is an hourly time-averaged rate. The fine reference provides precipitation-rate snapshots every 30 minutes. For an hour centered at $n$, the target is the trapezoidal approximation
\begin{equation}
\overline P_n=\tfrac14 P_{n-30\,\mathrm{min}}
+\tfrac12 P_n+\tfrac14 P_{n+30\,\mathrm{min}}.
\label{eq:hourly}
\end{equation}
Rates in kg\,m$^{-2}$\,s$^{-1}$ are multiplied by 3600 to express water-equivalent rates in $\rainunit$. Equation~\eqref{eq:hourly} approximates an hourly mean from three snapshots; it does not recover unobserved sub-half-hour variability. The remaining five target variables use midpoint snapshots and consequently retain a temporal-support mismatch with hourly mean predictors.

The accumulated precipitation collection is excluded. In the documented experiment, its accumulation/reset interval is not one hour, so reading successive hourly files as independent hourly totals would misdefine the target. The v4.1 experiment uses the separately prepared hourly-rate reference in Eq.~\eqref{eq:hourly}. Future-time reference samples enter this target construction only; no future coarse predictor or high-resolution state is used as a model input.

\subsection{Predictors, surface information, and history}
Coarse fields are bilinearly interpolated to the LCC grid. The dynamic predictor set contains total precipitation, the two 10-m wind components, total column water vapor, 2-m specific humidity, and sea-level pressure. Current coarse baselines for the original five target variables are also supplied through the inherited conditioning representation. The six dynamic predictors from the preceding hour are appended to both local and broad-context inputs. CAPE is not included.

Static conditions describe elevation, latitude and longitude, grid-cell area, surface fractions, coastline distance, and terrain slopes. Annual phase, UTC hour, and local solar hour are represented by sine--cosine pairs. Predictor normalization and residual statistics are fitted on training data. The previous-hour fields use the same normalization as their current-hour counterparts. High-resolution humidity and other reference states are used for supervision, not as previous-time inputs.

The task is to sample
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{Y}_n^{(m)}\sim p_\theta(\mathbf{Y}_n\mid
\mathbf{C}_n,\mathbf{C}_{n-1},\mathbf{S},\boldsymbol\tau_n),
\label{eq:conditional}
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{C}$ denotes coarse information, $\mathbf{S}$ static fields, and $\boldsymbol\tau$ time encodings. The distribution is conditional on contemporary large-scale information. It is not an independently initialized lead-time forecast. Similarly, generating each hour from a separate random draw does not by itself define coherent member trajectories through time.

\subsection{Chronological partitioning and provenance}
The annual configuration requests training data from 1 December 2024 through 30 November 2025, validation from 3 December 2025 through 31 January 2026, and testing from 3 February through 8 March 2026. The end dates are exclusive in the split configuration, and two-day gaps separate the main partitions. Eligible samples must have complete hourly precipitation targets, humidity targets, and previous-hour predictors within the same split. Initial hours and gaps are excluded when those conditions are not met.

These are configured intervals; final retained hour counts must be obtained from the production manifests and are not inferred from calendar duration. One full annual cycle in training helps expose the network to seasonal variation. The held-out period nevertheless covers only late winter and early spring, so it cannot support an all-season generalization claim. The preprocessing checks source timestamps, units, geometry, and finite values, and records fingerprints for the prepared archive and auxiliary target caches. Shared preprocessing statistics and the frozen regression checkpoint must be verified against the same training-only partition before final evaluation.

\figslot{4.0cm}{Domain, temporal sampling, and data support. Reserved for the LCC domain and terrain, native GEOS-FP cells, chronological train/validation/test intervals, and precipitation time windows. Show retained rather than merely requested sample counts, and mark the three exploratory test cases separately. Figure interior intentionally blank.}{fig:data}

\section{Conditional flow-matching method}\label{sec:method}
\subsection{A hybrid direct and residual representation}
Let $B_c$ be the coarse baseline for channel $c$, and let $R_c$ be the output of a pretrained deterministic regression model in physical units. For temperature, pressure, and the wind components, the learned endpoint is a scaled departure from $R_c$:
\begin{equation}
X_{1,c}=\frac{Y_c-R_c}{a_c},
\qquad c\in\{T,p,u,v\}.
\label{eq:state}
\end{equation}
The regression network is frozen while the flow model trains. Its prediction remains an additional conditioning field, so the stochastic model can use both the large-scale predictors and the estimated local state. The regression estimate is an operational reference and is not assumed to equal the exact conditional mean.

The inherited regression has no humidity output. Humidity therefore uses
\begin{equation}
X_{1,q}=\frac{Y_q-B_q}{a_q}.
\label{eq:q}
\end{equation}
The positive scales $a_c$ combine inherited training-only residual units with a training-batch root-mean-square calibration. Humidity initially uses $10^{-3}$ kg\,kg$^{-1}$ as its residual unit. Calibration is performed before flow optimization and stored in the checkpoint. It rescales the learning problem; it does not calibrate predictive probabilities.

Precipitation uses the direct transformation
\begin{equation}
X_{1,P}=g(P)=\sqrt{1+P/P_0}-1,
\quad P_0=1~\rainunit.
\label{eq:rain}
\end{equation}
Neither coarse nor regression precipitation is subtracted. Both remain available as conditioning information, allowing the generator to relocate or alter precipitation without requiring its output to be a residual added to a fixed rain field. This choice reduces the dynamic range while preserving a continuous transition at zero. There is no separate wet/dry classifier, dry-margin offset, or positive wet threshold in the v4.1 representation.

After generation, $z=\max(X_{1,P},0)$ is decoded as
\begin{equation}
\widehat P=P_0z(z+2).
\label{eq:decode}
\end{equation}
The decoder enforces nonnegative precipitation and has no upper cap. Other channels are decoded by adding their residuals to the relevant reference. Humidity is clipped to $[0,1]$ kg\,kg$^{-1}$. This broad bound prevents invalid mass fractions but is not a saturation constraint. No moisture, momentum, or energy conservation law follows from these transformations.



\subsection{Flow path and training objective}
For each paired training example, draw an independent standard normal field $X_0$ and a flow time $t\sim\mathcal U(0,1)$. The linear probability path and its conditional velocity are
\begin{align}
X_t&=(1-t)X_0+tX_1,\\
U_t&=X_1-X_0.
\end{align}
A neural field $v_\theta(X_t,t;C)$ predicts $U_t$ from the interpolated state, flow time, and all available conditions $C$. With pixel area $A_i$ and channel weights $w_c$, the implemented objective is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal L=\E\left[
\frac{1}{\sum_c w_c}\sum_c w_c
\frac{\sum_i A_i\left(v_{\theta,c,i}-U_{t,c,i}\right)^2}
{\sum_i A_i}\right].
\label{eq:loss}
\end{equation}
The expectation includes the time and patch sampling distribution. Weights in target order $(T,P,p,u,v,q)$ are $(1,2,1,1,1,1)$, giving precipitation twice the channel weight. All pixels in the input patch, including the halo, are supervised. The base v4.1 loss is quadratic; optional fine-tuning objectives elsewhere in the repository are not part of this base formulation.

For positive fixed channel and area weights, the population minimizer of the pointwise regression is the conditional expectation of the path velocity \citep{lipman2023,liu2023}. This statement explains the training objective but does not guarantee exact sampling with a finite model, finite training data, or a tiled velocity approximation. The linear conditional path also does not imply that a learned unconditional trajectory is straight or accurately integrated in one step.

At sampling time the learned ordinary differential equation is
\begin{equation}
\frac{\mathrm d X_t}{\mathrm dt}=v_\theta(X_t,t;C),
\qquad X_0\sim\mathcal N(0,I).
\label{eq:ode}
\end{equation}
Different initial noise fields produce ensemble members conditional on the same coarse state. They represent variability learned from the paired archive, not uncertainty in the supplied GEOS-FP analysis or in the training-model formulation.

\subsection{Multiscale conditioning network}
The velocity network is a conditional U-Net, following the multiresolution encoder--decoder principle of \citet{ronneberger2015}. The local input has a $128\times128$ nominal core with a 32-pixel halo on each side, giving $192\times192$ pixels. A surrounding $576\times576$ context region is reduced to $96\times96$ by area-mode interpolation, which averages blocks in grid-index space. This pooling is distinct from explicit physical grid-cell-area weighting in Eq.~\eqref{eq:loss}.

The network has base width 64 and channel multipliers $(1,2,3,4)$, with two residual blocks per level. Flow time is encoded in a 256-dimensional embedding. Conditioning fields enter the input and each encoder resolution. At the bottleneck, eight-head self-attention models relationships within the local representation, and cross-attention connects it to an $8\times8$ token representation of broad context. The decoder uses interpolated upsampling, skip connections, and residual blocks to return a six-channel velocity field. The frozen regression uses its original inputs and checkpoint architecture, preserving the meaning of its predictions.

The broad context is intended to provide synoptic and mesoscale information beyond the local receptive field. Static terrain and coastlines supply geographically fixed structure, while recent coarse conditions provide limited information about evolving weather. These are design motivations; their incremental value requires the ablation experiments described in Section~\ref{sec:ablation}.

\subsection{Patch sampling and optimization}
Training draws 32,768 timestamp--patch samples per epoch. Spatial sampling mixes a uniform component with a coarse-input proposal that emphasizes precipitation and coastal detail; the configured mixture gives each component one half of the probability mass. High-resolution target intensity does not determine the proposal. Sample weights remain one, so this scheme intentionally modifies the population of training inputs rather than estimating a uniformly sampled spatial risk through inverse-probability weights. Uniform held-out sampling is used for validation.

The experimental settings are summarized in the reserved Table~\ref{tab:settings}. The configured schedule uses 250 epochs, four GPUs, and 16 examples per GPU per update, giving an effective batch size of 64 and 512 optimizer updates per epoch. AdamW \citep{loshchilov2019} uses learning rate $2\times10^{-4}$, weight decay 0.01, a 2000-update linear warmup, and cosine decay to 5\% of the peak rate. Gradients are clipped at norm 1. Mixed bfloat16 precision is used for network computation, and an exponential moving average (EMA) with decay 0.9995 supplies evaluation weights.

Validation uses 256 fixed uniformly selected patches, eight members, and 24 Heun steps. It occurs every five epochs through epoch 20 and every two epochs thereafter in the checked-in configuration. The lowest validation precipitation CRPS defines the best checkpoint. This selection criterion does not guarantee that the same checkpoint optimizes every state variable. The preliminary diagnostic discussed below instead uses the latest checkpoint at epoch 243; it must not be relabeled as the validation-best model.

Reserved for the frozen regression provenance; target representations; local, halo, and context dimensions; network widths; optimizer and EMA settings; patch proposal; checkpoint selection; and validation/test sampler settings. Separate configured defaults from the settings recorded in the evaluated checkpoint and diagnostic archive. Table body intentionally blank.}{tab:settings}

\subsection{Synchronized tiled generation}
Independent patch integration would allow overlapping patches to follow incompatible stochastic paths. The full-domain sampler instead initializes a single padded six-channel Gaussian field. At each flow time, every tile reads its portion of the same state. Its predicted velocity is placed back into the common domain using an overlapping Hann window. If $E_k$ extracts tile $k$, $E_k^\top$ inserts it, and $W_k$ is its window, the assembled velocity is
\begin{equation}
V_\theta(X,t)=
\frac{\sum_k E_k^\top\!\left[W_kv_\theta(E_kX,t;C_k)\right]}
{\sum_k E_k^\top W_k}.
\label{eq:blend}
\end{equation}
Division is pixelwise. The configured core stride is 96 pixels, and the implementation checks that every padded-domain pixel has positive accumulated weight. A second-order explicit Heun scheme evaluates Eq.~\eqref{eq:blend} at both stages of every integration step. Frozen regression fields are blended consistently before residual decoding.

This procedure synchronizes overlapping trajectories, but it does not prove that all tile-conditioned distributions are compatible with a single exact global distribution. Boundary artifacts, overlap-induced smoothing, and dependence on tile placement remain empirical questions. The reference sampler keeps assembled fields in host memory and evaluates tiles on the accelerator. The faster batched evaluator caches conditions and assembled states on the device; its activation cost is controlled by tile batch size, but its cached storage grows with domain size. Device memory, host memory, and runtime therefore require measurement for the actual evaluation path.

\subsection{Packed training archive in v4.1}
Version 4.1 changes the data-access path while retaining the v4 model, targets, calibration, and base objective. Large context crops are pooled once per hour instead of reconstructed for every training example. Local fields are packed into tiled storage, and proposal probabilities are stored in a shared memory-mapped table. Persistent workers reuse mappings across epochs.

Candidate origins use a 24-pixel stride, divisible by the context-pooling factor six. For aligned origins, the packed and original loaders are designed to return identical tensors, including replicated boundary values. The terminal candidate on each axis is snapped down to a multiple of six; accordingly, the candidate population differs slightly from the earlier loader. Local tests compare real dataset-construction paths on synthetic inputs, and production preparation includes an equality check. These checks address numerical equivalence, not generalization or end-to-end speed on the production filesystem. The final computational assessment should report measured throughput, storage, and inference cost separately.

\subsection{Spectral processing in the exploratory diagnostics}\label{sec:spectral}
The available presentation archive evaluates an additional spectral operation after generation. It estimates reference spectra from 12 sampled archive hours, preferentially from the training split, near the calendar date of the first case. The resulting climatology is reused across the three evaluated cases. The implementation can fall back to other same-split hours if no training pool is available; a publication run must verify and record that the training-only branch was used.

For each generated member, radial spectral amplitudes are raised toward the reference climatology with gain capped at 1.3. The gain transitions from zero enhancement at a wavelength of 40 km to its full value at 20 km and shorter wavelengths. Fields are mirror-extended before the Fourier operation, and Fourier phases are retained. Precipitation is processed in $\sqrt{P}$ space, which differs from the training transformation in Eq.~\eqref{eq:rain}, and is then clipped and squared. Humidity is clipped again after processing.

This operation changes the evaluated predictive distribution. Preserving the zero-frequency component of a transformed field does not ensure preservation of precipitation amount after nonlinear decoding and clipping. Consequently, probability calibration, rain bias, and multivariate dependence must be checked after processing. All numerical results in Section~\ref{sec:results} refer to the processed pipeline. The available summary cannot attribute improvements separately to flow generation and spectral adjustment. The complete-test protocol in Section~\ref{sec:fulltest} addresses this with paired raw and processed ensembles.

\section{Evaluation design}\label{sec:evaluation}
\subsection{References and matched comparisons}
The basic comparisons are interpolated GEOS-FP, the frozen deterministic regression, individual flow members, and the ensemble mean. All quantitative comparisons use the same fine-grid reference cells and timestamps. Native coarse-grid maps may be shown for visual context, but scores use the interpolated coarse baseline. For humidity, the ``regression'' comparator is identical to coarse QV2M because the inherited regression has no humidity channel.

The primary scientific comparison should be an unprocessed, validation-selected flow against both coarse input and frozen regression. The spectral variant is a separate pipeline. A broader assessment should also include a deterministic model with matched predictor history and capacity, and a competitive conditional generative baseline trained on the same examples. Without those controls, the current comparisons measure the value of the complete available pipeline, not an isolated advantage of flow matching over diffusion or other generators.

The saved diagnostic contains 7 March 2026 at 19:30 UTC, selected as a wettest test case, and two randomly selected hours: 9 February 2026 at 20:30 UTC and 6 March 2026 at 17:30 UTC. Each has eight ensemble members and 64 Heun steps using EMA weights at epoch 243. One selected wet case plus two random cases is not a representative random sample of the full test distribution. All averages below give the cases equal weight, matching the saved report.

\subsection{Complete held-out evaluation protocol}\label{sec:fulltest}
A separate full-archive evaluator enumerates every eligible test hour, records indexed hours excluded by the target/history requirements, and snapshots the selected checkpoint before generation. The planned reference run uses the validation-best EMA checkpoint, 16 members, and 64 Heun steps. It evaluates all six variables and wind speed derived from each member's wind components. Missing or failed hours prevent the final output from being labeled complete; per-hour results allow interrupted runs to resume without reselecting cases.

The raw and spectrally processed ensembles use identical noise draws. The spectral reference is fixed before sampling and must contain training hours only; its dates and checksum are retained. This design measures the incremental effect of filtering without changing the neural weights or stochastic realizations. Coarse input and frozen regression are scored on the same grid and hours. Fair CRPS, ranks, coverage, spectra, and gradient diagnostics are retained for every variable, with precipitation occurrence, reliability, and neighborhood verification added separately.

For this complete evaluation, RMSE is computed by pooling area-weighted squared errors over hours before taking the square root. Reliability, contingency statistics, and FSS likewise pool their numerator and denominator terms. This convention differs from the mean of hourly RMSE in the exploratory presentation record. Paired differences in CRPS, MSE, and bias use temporal-block bootstrap intervals, initially with 72-hour blocks and a planned sensitivity to block length. All-domain scores include ocean cells. This protocol is implemented but has not yet been executed on the production archive; the numerical results below remain the three-case diagnostics.

\subsection{Deterministic and probabilistic scores}
For field $c$, let $\bar Y_i=M^{-1}\sum_m Y_i^{(m)}$ and let $y_i$ denote the fine reference. At a given hour, area-weighted RMSE is
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{RMSE}=\left[\frac{\sum_i A_i(\bar Y_i-y_i)^2}{\sum_i A_i}\right]^{1/2}.
\end{equation}
MAE and bias replace the squared error with its absolute or signed value. The diagnostic averages these per-hour statistics; averaging RMSE across cases differs from taking the square root of a pooled mean-square error. Spatial correlation is supplementary because large-scale gradients can dominate it.

The empirical continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) at a pixel is
\begin{align}
\mathrm{CRPS}_M={}&\frac1M\sum_{m=1}^M|Y^{(m)}-y|\nonumber\\
&-\frac{1}{2M^2}\sum_{m=1}^M\sum_{l=1}^M|Y^{(m)}-Y^{(l)}|.
\label{eq:crps}
\end{align}
CRPS evaluates an entire predictive distribution and reduces to absolute error for a deterministic prediction \citep{gneiting2007}. Scores are area-averaged, then averaged across cases. Relative skill against a reference is
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{CRPSS}=1-\frac{\langle\mathrm{CRPS}_{\rm model}\rangle}
{\langle\mathrm{CRPS}_{\rm reference}\rangle}.
\end{equation}
Thus comparison with deterministic GEOS-FP uses its MAE in the denominator. This is a valid score comparison, but a positive CRPSS does not imply improvement in ensemble-mean RMSE.

The saved evaluator uses Eq.~\eqref{eq:crps}, not a finite-ensemble fair score. Comparisons across ensemble sizes should also report the fair CRPS, replacing $M^2$ by $M(M-1)$ in the pairwise term, under the appropriate exchangeability and independent-sampling assumptions \citep{ferro2014}. Both the number of members and the estimator must accompany reported scores. Fair scores cannot be reconstructed from the aggregate scorecard alone without its underlying pairwise statistics or ensemble fields.

\subsection{Calibration and spatial structure}
Calibration is assessed with randomized-tie rank histograms, empirical central intervals, and spread relative to ensemble-mean error. Ties matter especially for dry precipitation. The saved central interval uses the interpolated empirical 5th and 95th percentiles of only eight members. Its coverage should be interpreted with finite-ensemble sampling in mind, rather than as a perfectly resolved 90\% interval. Low coverage across several variables nevertheless identifies a practical limitation requiring larger-ensemble verification and calibration analysis.

Pointwise scores do not evaluate whether rainbands or fronts have realistic spatial dependence. Precipitation verification therefore includes the fractions skill score (FSS) \citep{roberts2008}, event reliability, Brier score, intensity distributions, and threshold contingency statistics. For neighborhood exceedance fractions $f_i$ and $o_i$,
\begin{equation}
\mathrm{FSS}=1-\frac{\sum_i A_i(f_i-o_i)^2}
{\sum_i A_i(f_i^2+o_i^2)}.
\end{equation}
Only centers with complete neighborhoods are included; a zero denominator is reported as undefined. Individual-member and ensemble-mean FSS answer different questions and should be reported separately. The configured diagnostic thresholds span light to heavy precipitation, while threshold-specific sample counts are essential for interpreting rare events.

Radially averaged Fourier power, spatial gradients, and front-normal profiles describe spatial texture. Spectra are computed after mean removal and Hann tapering in LCC index space. Converting frequency to kilometers with a representative cell scale is approximate and should be accompanied by the actual geometry. Spectral agreement alone is not a proper probabilistic verification criterion: displaced or phase-randomized structures can have similar power. Multivariate evaluation should additionally examine joint extremes, cross-variable relationships, and a variogram-based score \citep{scheuerer2015}. These latter tests are requirements for a complete assessment and are not claimed as completed here.

\subsection{Uncertainty and ablation strategy}\label{sec:ablation}
Uncertainty intervals for aggregate skill should resample independent weather episodes or sufficiently long temporal blocks, retaining the paired comparisons between methods. Treating approximately 1.9 million spatially correlated cells as independent samples would create misleading precision. The current three cases do not support stable block-bootstrap confidence intervals or seasonal significance tests; none are reported.

The core ablations should remove one design component at a time: previous-hour predictors, broad context, residual regression conditioning, or the precipitation-specific representation. Additional tests should compare uniform with input-weighted patch proposals, alternative channel weights, and synchronized versus independently integrated tiles. Sampler convergence should be evaluated with multiple integration-step counts and shifted tile layouts. Spectral processing requires matched raw and processed members generated from the same initial noise. Each choice must be selected on validation data before a new untouched test evaluation; repeatedly ranking recipes on the same test cases converts them into development cases.

Reserved for the matched held-out comparison of interpolated GEOS-FP, frozen regression, a capacity-matched deterministic model, unprocessed flow, and spectrally processed flow. Report per-variable MAE, RMSE, bias, empirical and fair CRPS where available, ensemble size, and episode-block uncertainty intervals. Label three-case exploratory results separately from a complete test-set evaluation. Table body intentionally blank.}{tab:scores}

\section{Preliminary results}\label{sec:results}
\subsection{Probabilistic scores improve on the selected cases}
The comparison is organized in Table~\ref{tab:scores} and Fig.~\ref{fig:skill}, whose interiors are reserved for the completed evaluation. The saved three-case scorecard reports positive empirical CRPS skill relative to interpolated GEOS-FP for all six variables. Precipitation CRPS decreases from 0.1098 to 0.0874 $\rainunit$, a 20.4\% improvement. Temperature decreases from 0.965 to 0.597 K (38.2\%), surface pressure from 4.655 to 0.212 hPa (95.5\%), and the zonal and meridional wind components from 0.769 to 0.551 and from 0.797 to 0.560 $\windunit$ (28.3\% and 29.8\%). Specific humidity decreases from 0.336 to 0.240 $\qrate$ (28.6\%). These are ratios of case-mean scores and apply only to the identified processed ensemble.

The comparison with frozen regression is also informative. Its deterministic MAE is 0.1042 $\rainunit$ for precipitation and 0.7547 K for temperature, compared with ensemble CRPS of 0.0874 $\rainunit$ and 0.5969 K. This suggests that the stochastic predictions contain useful distributional information beyond the inherited deterministic estimate on these cases. It does not isolate model capacity, additional history, or spectral processing, and it does not establish significance across weather regimes. The large pressure improvement should likewise be interpreted against the specific coarse baseline and model-reference relationship rather than as a universal accuracy gain.

\figslot{3.8cm}{Probabilistic and deterministic comparison. Reserved for per-variable CRPS skill and ensemble-mean RMSE relative to interpolated GEOS-FP and frozen regression. Use physical units, display individual cases alongside the three-case average, and distinguish processed from unprocessed ensembles. Add uncertainty intervals only after an appropriately sized held-out evaluation. Figure interior intentionally blank.}{fig:skill}

\subsection{Precipitation probability skill does not imply lower RMSE}
Precipitation ensemble-mean RMSE is 0.6828 $\rainunit$, compared with 0.6727 $\rainunit$ for interpolated GEOS-FP, an increase of approximately 1.5\%. The frozen regression gives 0.6923 $\rainunit$. Ensemble-mean MAE is 0.1111 $\rainunit$, also slightly above the coarse value of 0.1098 $\rainunit$, and the mean precipitation bias is $-0.0412$ $\rainunit$. The first ensemble member has RMSE 0.7978 $\rainunit$.

These results are compatible with a distribution that improves a probabilistic score without improving the expected point estimate. Individual realizations may place plausible precipitation differently from the single reference realization; averaging members can reduce displacement-related error while also smoothing structure. The reported scores do not establish that the generated fine-scale precipitation is located correctly. Neighborhood scores, intensity-conditioned verification, and event displacement diagnostics are needed to separate placement, amplitude, and occurrence errors.

The negative domain-mean precipitation bias is a further reason to assess amounts explicitly. A transform designed to stabilize training and a spectral operation designed to enhance texture need not preserve physical rain volume. Wet-area fraction, high quantiles, and area-integrated amounts should therefore be examined jointly. Neither visual sharpness nor a lower CRPS substitutes for these checks.

\subsection{State-variable errors have different sources}
For temperature, ensemble-mean RMSE falls from 1.558 to 1.245 K, approximately a 20.0\% reduction relative to coarse input. It is close to the frozen-regression RMSE of 1.242 K. On these cases, much of the deterministic temperature correction is already present in the regression, while the ensemble improves the distributional score. This distinction is consistent with the rationale for a regression-conditioned generator, although the aggregate metrics do not identify the physical source of each correction.

Surface-pressure RMSE decreases from 10.344 to 0.376 hPa; the frozen regression yields 0.976 hPa. Surface pressure is sensitive to terrain height, so a large improvement over interpolated coarse surface pressure may include systematic adjustment to the reference orography. The present data do not separate terrain-related and synoptic-pressure errors. A stronger evaluation should stratify error by elevation and terrain mismatch and include a simple terrain-aware pressure baseline. It should also verify decoding, units, and grid alignment before interpreting the magnitude as meteorological skill.

The ensemble-mean RMSE of the zonal wind component decreases from 1.090 to 1.048 $\windunit$, and that of the meridional component decreases from 1.087 to 1.020 $\windunit$. The frozen regression has slightly lower RMSE than the ensemble mean for both components, at 1.033 and 1.001 $\windunit$. Humidity RMSE decreases from 0.511 to 0.461 $\qrate$, with a remaining mean dry bias of $-0.171$ $\qrate$. Joint evaluation of temperature and humidity is needed to determine whether individually improved fields also describe credible near-surface thermodynamic states.

\subsection{Ensemble coverage remains limited}
Figure~\ref{fig:calibration} reserves the calibration diagnostics. Central empirical 90\% interval coverage is 76.1\% for precipitation, 60.5\% for temperature, 69.3\% for surface pressure, 65.3\% and 65.5\% for the wind components, and 57.1\% for humidity. The saved finite-member-adjusted spread-to-error diagnostics also remain below one, spanning approximately 0.55--0.84. Together, these diagnostics are consistent with insufficient dispersion and/or residual bias relative to the high-resolution reference.

Eight members provide coarse resolution of tail probabilities, and interpolated sample quantiles are not calibrated population quantiles even under ideal sampling. Consequently, the difference from nominal coverage should not be attributed entirely to model underdispersion. Nevertheless, the observed coverage is too low to describe these intervals as validated uncertainty estimates. Increasing ensemble size, examining rank histograms by regime, and separating systematic bias from insufficient conditional variability are immediate priorities.

A satisfactory aggregate CRPS does not remove this concern. CRPS balances distributional location and spread, whereas interval coverage interrogates a particular aspect of calibration. Users requiring threshold exceedance probabilities or joint weather scenarios need both discrimination and reliability. The current evidence supports neither calibrated tail-risk estimation nor a claim that ensemble spread includes all relevant uncertainty sources.

Show raw and spectrally processed ensembles, report member count, and distinguish unconditional aggregates from precipitation-intensity, terrain, and weather-regime strata. Figure interior intentionally blank.}{fig:calibration}

\subsection{Organized precipitation and the remaining moisture challenge}\label{sec:qualitative}
Qualitative comparisons during model development indicated that generating the complete precipitation field produced more organized storm structures and precipitation patterns than the earlier residual-rain configuration. For the smoother state variables, residual correction remains a useful way to retain the regression estimate while adding conditional variability. These observations motivate the hybrid representation; they are distinct from the three-case numerical comparison with coarse input reported above.

A residual formulation is mathematically capable of changing a storm's location and amplitude, so the qualitative finding should not be interpreted as an intrinsic impossibility of residual precipitation generation. Differences in target transforms, temporal averaging, conditioning, and training can also change storm structure. The decisive experiment holds the v4.1 architecture, data, hourly reference, optimizer, and sampling budget fixed while comparing $g(P)$ with $g(P)-g(R_P)$. Raw-model comparisons should precede filtering, with identical verification and independently selected checkpoints.

Bounded spectral processing made details visually sharper in development examples without retraining the model. Fine moisture structures remained more difficult to reproduce. This motivates a variable-by-variable assessment of gradient strength and spectral power, rather than judging success from the most visually striking precipitation or wind panel. The reported humidity CRPS gain and dry bias concern probability and amplitude; neither establishes that humidity fronts are sharp or correctly located. The complete evaluator records humidity spectra, gradient percentiles, CRPS, bias, and coverage together, allowing a sharpening gain to be assessed against its statistical costs.

\subsection{Spatial realism and computational evidence}
The local archive contains case maps and diagnostic graphics, but the aggregate numerical summary used here does not establish threshold-specific spatial skill, unbiased spectra, or a quantified reduction in tile artifacts. We therefore make no numerical claim about FSS, front widths, extremes, or spectral recovery. The reserved spatial figure (Fig.~\ref{fig:spatial}) should compare native coarse information, interpolated input, the model reference, several members, and the ensemble mean with common color scales. This design reveals both added variability and the smoothing induced by ensemble averaging.

The presentation record reports approximately 3822 s for the complete diagnostic run. That time includes generation and presentation tasks, so it is not a clean sampler benchmark. Similarly, the packed-loader design reduces repeated reads by construction, but local microbenchmarks and estimated file sizes cannot establish production speedup over a dynamical model. Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reserves the sensitivity and cost assessment. An end-to-end comparison requires hardware, software, data-cache state, ensemble size, integration steps, and output scope to be held explicit. No speedup or energy-efficiency factor is claimed here.

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
\subsection{What the hybrid representation contributes}
The method combines two useful statistical decompositions (Fig.~\ref{fig:workflow}) within the regression-to-generative strategy introduced for atmospheric downscaling by CorrDiff. For the state variables, a deterministic reference supplies predictable structure and the flow describes remaining conditional variability. For precipitation, direct generation removes residual subtraction and add-back from the learning coordinates, while retaining coarse and regression rain as conditions. The shared network can still use deterministic rain information as a condition. This is a practical extension of regression-conditioned generative downscaling, rather than a claim that residual generation or conditional flow matching is itself new.

The direct rain transform also changes the balance of errors. Its compression makes intense rates less dominant in transformed-space regression, while its inverse expands positive errors nonlinearly in physical units. The precipitation channel weight partly adjusts the relative training emphasis but does not optimize CRPS directly. The observed combination of improved CRPS, slightly degraded RMSE, and negative bias is therefore a substantive evaluation outcome, not a contradiction to be resolved by selecting a single favorable score.

Joint generation is attractive because applications often require internally related fields rather than independent marginal samples. However, sharing network features is not equivalent to enforcing thermodynamic consistency. A generated humid warm field and its wind pattern can each have plausible marginal statistics while their joint relationship is wrong. Physical diagnostics and multivariate scores are needed before using these ensembles for compound-event assessment or coupled impact simulations.

\subsection{Limits of the paired model reference}
The experiment measures fidelity to a high-resolution GEOS replay conditioned on GEOS-FP. Shared dynamics, parameterizations, and large-scale constraints can make this problem easier than transfer to an independent model or observational analysis. Conversely, reference-model errors can be learned faithfully. A low pressure or temperature error against the reference is not automatically a low error against stations.

Independent validation should match spatial and temporal support as closely as possible. Precipitation comparisons need accumulation windows that correspond to Eq.~\eqref{eq:hourly}; point gauges and gridded radar estimates also introduce representativeness and measurement errors. Temperature, humidity, and wind require attention to sensor height, elevation, exposure, and land-surface characteristics. Such validation would answer a different question from the present model-emulation task and should be reported separately.

The temporal partition further restricts interpretation. The test window lacks a warm-season convective sample, while the training archive covers only one annual cycle. Rare-event behavior, climate nonstationarity, and robustness to changes in the operational GEOS system remain unresolved. Extension to MERRA-2 or a longer retrospective product would require new paired preparation, checks for distribution shift, and independent verification; the project name alone establishes none of these capabilities.

\subsection{Calibration, sharpening, and selection}
The preliminary ensemble is informative relative to deterministic baselines under empirical CRPS, yet its uncertainty intervals remain too narrow or biased in the selected cases. Similar calibration difficulties have been identified in generative atmospheric downscaling \citep{mardani2025}. Remedies should be evaluated against proper scores and reliability on untouched data, rather than tuned only for visual detail. Increasing sample count improves estimation of the learned distribution but does not correct a misspecified distribution.

Spectral processing deserves particular care. It changes amplitude statistics using a climatological target, but does not observe the true fine-scale phase of the evaluated event. A climatologically plausible spectrum can therefore coexist with misplaced precipitation, altered totals, or incorrect dependence across channels. The raw flow and the processed pipeline must be evaluated separately, with any spectral climatology restricted to training data and its source dates retained. If recipes were selected using the three current cases, an additional independent test sample is required.

Choosing checkpoints by precipitation CRPS is transparent, but it gives one variable priority. The six-variable objective should ultimately be judged by a preregistered set of per-field and joint criteria. For example, an improvement in precipitation probability skill may be unacceptable if it substantially degrades thermodynamic coherence or wind extremes. Such decisions depend on the intended application and should not be hidden inside a single composite score.

\subsection{Scalability and temporal extension}
Synchronized tiled integration provides a practical compromise between local learning and continental output. It retains common stochastic states in overlaps while allowing accelerator-sized computation. The packed archive addresses a separate bottleneck: moving enough examples through a shared filesystem to keep training hardware active. Neither technique removes the need to measure full-domain cost, host-memory demand, and numerical convergence.

The current model conditions on previous-hour coarse weather but generates each valid time independently. It therefore cannot be assumed to preserve member identity, storm motion, or physically realistic hour-to-hour precipitation intermittency. Spatiotemporal generators, including video-diffusion approaches \citep{srivastava2024}, motivate a future trajectory-level formulation. Any such extension should evaluate multihour accumulations, temporal spectra, and event propagation rather than infer temporal realism from smooth animations alone.

Global coverage is a planned extension. The present experiments, implementation assessment, and scientific claims are restricted to CONUS.

\section{Conclusions}\label{sec:conclusion}
We have presented \model{} v4.1, a conditional flow-matching system for joint kilometer-scale generation of six near-surface weather fields from GEOS-FP. The formulation uses direct square-root precipitation, regression-centered temperature, pressure and winds, and coarse-centered humidity. Local and broad-context conditioning connect surface information and recent coarse weather to a shared stochastic generator. Velocity blending at every Heun stage supports synchronized full-domain sampling, while a packed archive reduces repeated training-data preparation.

The available three-case processed ensemble improves empirical CRPS relative to interpolated GEOS-FP for every field, but precipitation ensemble-mean RMSE does not improve and interval coverage remains limited. These results support further study of the conditional representation while identifying calibration and precipitation amount and placement as central weaknesses. They do not establish all-season skill, observational superiority, or an advantage over other generative model classes.

The most distinctive research finding is the qualitative improvement in organized precipitation under direct generation, coupled with the remaining difficulty in sharpening moisture. A matched residual-rain ablation is needed to quantify that effect. The implemented complete-test evaluator provides the next assessment of all six fields and derived wind speed, including paired raw-versus-filtered predictions; its production results are not yet available. Matched generative baselines and spatial and multivariate verification remain necessary. A longer independent reference period and observational checks are required before this prototype can support broad claims about reliable high-resolution weather information.

\section*{Code and data availability}
The implementation described here is contained in the \model{} repository, including the v4.1 configuration, packed-data preparation, training, synchronized inference, and evaluation routines. The numerical evidence for this draft is the locally supplied three-case presentation summary and its associated case scorecards. A permanent public repository URL, tagged source release, archived configuration, checkpoint identifiers, and data-access documentation have not yet been supplied and must be added to the submission. The high-resolution GEOS replay archive is project-hosted on NASA NCCS Discover; no claim of unrestricted public availability is made. The final release should specify permissions and access procedures for source data and trained weights, and preserve training/test manifests and the spectral-reference dates.

\appendix
\section{Implementation details and reproducibility}\label{app:implementation}
\subsection{Exact residual construction}
The inherited archive stores residual means $\mu_c$ and standard deviations $s_c$. For a non-rain state channel, let $r_c$ be the frozen regression prediction in its standardized residual coordinates. The physical reference is
\begin{equation}
R_c=B_c+\mu_c+s_cr_c.
\end{equation}
With training-only calibration $\kappa_c$, the implemented flow target is
\begin{equation}
X_{1,c}=\frac{(Y_c-B_c-\mu_c)/s_c-r_c}{\kappa_c}.
\end{equation}
Thus Eq.~\eqref{eq:state} has $a_c=s_c\kappa_c$. For humidity, $r_q=\mu_q=0$, $s_q=10^{-3}$ kg\,kg$^{-1}$, and $R_q=B_q$. Calibration computes the area-weighted RMS of standardized residual errors over training-patch cores and applies a lower bound of 0.05 to $\kappa_c$. Precipitation is assigned calibration one and follows the direct representation independently.

The frozen regression's precipitation was learned in an inherited log-residual representation. Its output is decoded to physical rain and re-encoded into square-root space before being supplied as a conditioning channel. It is never added to generated rain. Confusing this conditioning path with the precipitation target path would describe a different model.

\subsection{Heun integration and physical decoding}
For $N$ uniform steps, $\Delta t=1/N$ and $t_j=j/N$. Using the assembled field in Eq.~\eqref{eq:blend},
\begin{align}
K_1&=V_\theta(X_j,t_j),\\
K_2&=V_\theta(X_j+\Delta tK_1,t_j+\Delta t),\\
X_{j+1}&=X_j+\tfrac{\Delta t}{2}(K_1+K_2).
\end{align}
Both stages synchronize tiles. Each step requires two network evaluations per tile. The final padded halo is removed, and decoding is performed on the assembled physical domain. Nonfinite intermediate states or decoded values fail explicitly. The default production configuration requests 32 steps and 16 members, whereas the presentation evidence uses 64 steps and eight members. These settings are distinct from the 24-step, eight-member patch validation used during training.

\subsection{Spectral operation}
Let $S_m(k)$ be a member's radial power and $S_{\rm tr}(k)$ the climatological reference power. The amplitude ratio is
\begin{equation}
G_0(k)=\operatorname{clip}\!\left(
\sqrt{S_{\rm tr}(k)/S_m(k)},1,1.3\right).
\end{equation}
Where member power is zero the implementation uses unit gain. With wavelength $\lambda$, the applied gain is
\begin{align}
a(\lambda)&=\operatorname{clip}\!\left(\frac{40-\lambda}{20},0,1\right),\\
G(k)&=1+a(\lambda(k))[G_0(k)-1].
\end{align}
Wavelengths are in kilometers and the zero-frequency gain is one. Gains are interpolated from radial bins onto the Fourier grid. The mirror-extended field is inverse-transformed and cropped. For precipitation, these operations act on $\sqrt{\max(P,0)}$ and the result is clipped and squared. This processing must be included in checkpoint-to-score provenance even though it does not change the trained neural weights.

\subsection{Required release record}
A reproducible release needs the source commit, exact frozen-regression and flow checkpoints, their hashes, resolved configuration, normalization and calibration arrays, input/target fingerprints, retained time manifests, member seeds, integration and tiling settings, and spectral-climatology dates. It also needs the exact verification mask and aggregation convention. Configuration files establish intended settings, but a saved checkpoint establishes the settings used in a particular run. Any discrepancy must be resolved before assigning experimental results to that configuration.

\section{Finite-ensemble interpretation}\label{app:finite}
For exchangeable independent members with finite variance, spread and ensemble-mean error have different finite-sample factors. If the implementation reports the population-normalized ensemble variance $s_M^2=M^{-1}\sum_m(Y^{(m)}-\bar Y)^2$, an approximate variance correction for comparison with ensemble-mean error multiplies $s_M^2$ by $(M+1)/(M-1)$. The saved presentation scorecard instead multiplies its reported spread-to-RMSE ratio by $\sqrt{(M+1)/M}$. Its stored spread uses population-normalized variance, so this is not the full correction just derived. We retain the saved convention when quoting its values; a final evaluation should report the variance convention explicitly and compute any correction consistently. Neither ratio proves calibration, and averaging casewise standard deviations before forming a ratio differs from pooling all second moments.

Similarly, the empirical CRPS and fair CRPS evaluate related but distinct finite-ensemble quantities. The former scores the issued empirical distribution directly; the latter removes the leading ensemble-size contribution to the expected pairwise term under independent sampling. Repeating the comparison for several member counts is particularly useful here because eight members poorly resolve tails. Reported coverage must specify the quantile convention, and rank ties must be randomized for precipitation.
